Preface
This databook describes the Synopsys DesignWare® Cores Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY.
Databook Organization
This databook is organized as follows:
■ Chapter 1, “Product Overview”, provides an introduction to the DesignWare Cores Die-to-Die 112G 
USR/XSR PHY and its features.
■ Chapter 2, “Deliverables”, describes the deliverables included in the PHY full-package release.
■ Chapter 3, “Signal Descriptions”, describes the PHY top-level signals.
■ Chapter 4, “Functional Description”, provides a functional overview of the PHY.
■ Chapter 5, “PHY Usage and Configuration”, explains the usage of PHY features and lists the PHY 
configuration settings for the protocols and data rates that are supported.
■ Chapter 6, “Test and Diagnostics”, details supported test procedures and diagnostic features.
■ Chapter 7, “Verilog Model Description”, provides an overview of simulating the PHY Verilog model.
■ Chapter 8, “Physical-Level Implementation”, describes the physical implementation of the PHY.
■ Chapter 9, “Package-Level Implementation”, describes requirements and guidelines for imple-
menting the PHY at the package level.
■ Chapter 10, “Reference Clock Implementation”, describes the PHY reference clock.
■ Chapter 11, “Register Descriptions”, details all possible registers in the PHY.
■ Appendix A, “PHY Configuration”, provides configuration values for configuring the PHY for your 
application.

1.2 General Product Description
The DesignWare Cores Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY meets the growing high bandwidth and low latency 
needs of high-performance data center applications. The PHY delivers signal integrity and jitter 
performance that targets to align with the CEI-112G-XSR and CEI-56G-USR standards electrical 
specifications. The PHY is small in area, low in power consumption, and high in performance, meeting the 
needs of die-to-die interconnects with channel losses up to 10dB. 
Figure 1-1 shows the Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY usage model, considering the following:
■ PHY: dwc_d2d_sr112_phy_tsmc12ffc_x8ns
■ TX PMA: dwc_d2d_sr112_tx_top_x8
■ RX PMA: dwc_d2d_sr112_rx_top_x8
Figure 1-1 PHY Usage Model
Power Consumption x8 @ 112 Gbps PAM4 Typical (preliminary)
■ TX Total Power per Lane: 103.70 mW per lane (0.93 pJ/b unidirectional power per lane)
■ RX Total Power per Lane: 95.58 mW per lane (0.85 pJ/b unidirectional power per lane)

1.3 Features
The PHY supports the following features:
■ Two independent macros. Unless stated otherwise, the information for the TX PMA and RX PMA is 
the same. The term PMA is used for both TX and RX PMAs.
❑ x8 TX macro
❑ x8 RX macro
■ Receiver: AFE containing ATT and CTLE with programmable boost
■ Transmitter: 3-tap digital FFE, low-power driver 
■ PAM-4 and NRZ signaling supported 
■ Supports the following baudrates (1.75 GSps to 112 GSps):
❑ Quarter Rate
❑ Half Rate 
❑ Full Rate 
❑ 2x oversampling 
❑ 4x oversampling 
■ PLL: Two cascaded PLLs, one LC-tank based and the other ring-oscillator based which enable gapless 
data rate support
■ Low jitter Phase-Locked Loops (PLLs)
■ Visibility and controllability of hard macro functions through programmable registers in the design:
❑ Overrides on all ASIC side functional control inputs for easy debug
❑ Access register space through a parallel interface
❑ Access register space through a JTAG port
■ Both forwarded clock and embedded clocking modes supported 
■ Extensive PMA debug capability through read/write and read-only registers in Raw_PCS
■ Configurable Raw_PCS with extensive debug options:
❑ Independent TX and RX (control per lane)
❑ Pseudo-random bit sequence (PRBS) generation and checker PRBS31, PRBS15, PRBS7
■ Register-based control of all Raw_PCS–to–PMA signals
■ Advanced, built-in diagnostics including on-chip eye scope
■ Die-to-Die channels for intra-package links 
■ Programmable 128-bit pattern generation with error injection capability
■ TX Total Power Consumption per Lane x8 @ 112Gbps PAM4 (preliminary)
❑ 103.70 mW per lane
❑ 0.93 pJ/b per lane (unidirectional)
■ RX Total Power Consumption per Lane x8 @ 112Gbps PAM4 (preliminary)
❑ 95.58 mW per lane
❑ 0.85 pJ/b per lane (unidirectional)

1.4 Applications
The PHY targets hyperscale data centers, enterprise and campus networks, cloud computing and 
networking, service provider networks, artificial intelligence, and machine learning.
The PHY enables designers to introduce competitive products using the latest standards because it is 
designed for low power, efficient area, low test costs, and process centering for optimal yield and 
interoperability.

2.1 Introduction
The full-package for each DesignWare Cores Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY release contains all of the 
design views and information required to instantiate the PHY in a top-level chip design. It also allows for 
the simulation of both the digital functionality of the PHY and the signal integrity of the link, and provides 
the information to enable production testing of the PHY.
Since the Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY consists of two independent TX and RX Macros, the PHY package 
includes front-end and back-end views for TX, as well as front-end and back-end views for RX. 
For information about the Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY release directory structure, see the top-level 
Readme file included in the product deliverables.
The deliverables are grouped into four basic categories based on usage:
■ Front-end view—Inclu des deliverables for system simulation and floor planning
■ Back-end view—Inclu des deliverables for physical design and finishing
■ Signal-integrity view—Includes de liverables for simulation of link performance at the analog level
■ Test view—Includes deli verables for testing
2.2 Front-End Views
Front-end views are provided for both the hard and soft macro portions of the XSR PHY.
A front-end view includes the following:
■ Verilog Testbench (VTB) examples
■ Digital simulation environment
■ RTL for soft macro 
■ GTECH netlist for hard macros
■ Synthesis timing models in .db and .lib formats
■ Behavioral models for analog blocks
■ Library Exchange Format (LEF) file with pin sizes and locations
■ Protocol-specific testbench examples
■ Synthesis constraints for the RAW PCS soft macro portion
■ IP-XACT XML file with register details
■ Spyglass views 
■ UPF views
■ DesignWare Cores Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR x8 PHY for TSMC 12-nm FFC/1.8V Databook
Note The packaged testbench is a sample testbench for you to use for reference and 
pipe-cleaning purposes. The testbench does not exhaustively include all 
validation tests for all protocols.
===== PAGE 33 =====
Synopsys, Inc. 
Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR x8 PHY for TSMC 12-nm FFC/1.8V Databook                                                         Deliverables
SolvNetPlus 
DesignWare

May 17, 2022
35
2.3 Back-End Views
Back-end views are provided for the hard macro portions of the TX and RX PHY. These deliverables tend to 
be metal stack dependent, therefore, multiple files for each hard macro are provided. The top-level cell 
name inside all of the files is the same, but the content is not—you must choose the appropriate file that 
matches your metal stack-up.
A back-end view includes the following:
■ GDSII layout and layer map files
■ Layout-Versus-Schematic (LVS) netlist in SPICE format and LVS report
■ LVS samples
■ Design Rule Check (DRC) report
■ DRC samples
■ Generic technology (GTECH) netlists and models for simulation
■ Gate-level Verilog
■ Gate-level netlist and Standard Delay Format (SDF) timing file
2.4 Signal-Integrity Views
A signal-integrity view includes the following:
■ S-Parameter models of RX and TX to facilitate package design
■ IBIS-AMI models of RX and TX for signal integrity simulations
■ Current waveforms, along with on-die power grid equivalent capacitance and resistance (Cdie and 
Rdie, respectively) for power delivery network simulations in the package
2.5 Test Views
Test view includes the following:
■ Scan test environment with Automatic Test Pattern Generation (ATPG)
■ Scan Verilog netlist
Note For more information about the deliverables, contact Synopsys.

3
Signal Descriptions
This chapter details all I/O signals in the IP. Inputs are on the left of the signal diagrams; outputs are on the 
right. In addition to describing the function of each signal, the signal descriptions in this chapter might 
include the following information:
■ Active State: Indicates whether the signal is active high or active low. When a signal is not intended 
to be used in a particular application, then this signal needs to be tied or driven to the inactive state 
(opposite of the active state).
■ Registered: Indicates whether or not the signal is registered directly inside the IP boundary without 
intervening logic (excluding simple buffers). A value of No does not imply that the signal is not 
synchronous, only that there is some combinatorial logic between the signal's origin or destination 
register and the boundary of the IP. A value of N/A indicates that this information is not provided for 
this IP title.
■ Synchronous to: Indicates which clocks in the IP sample this input (drive for an output). This clock 
might not be the same as the clock that your application logic should use to clock (sample/drive) this 
pin. For more details, consult the clock section in the databook.
The I/O signals are grouped as follows:
■ “Reset and Clock Signals” on page 39
■ “APB Interface Signals” on page 41
■ “Miscellaneous Configuration and Status Signals” on page 43
■ “JTAG Signals” on page 44
■ “Interrupt Signals” on page 45
■ “CPU Signals” on page 46
■ “Closely-Coupled Memories AHB-Lite Slave Interface Signals” on page 49
■ “Data Interface Signals” on page 51
■ “Analog Signals” on page 53
■ “Pad Signals” on page 54
■ “Resistor Tune Signals (SUP block)” on page 56
■ “TX - EQ Signals” on page 57
■ “DFT - Scan Signals” on page 58

PHY during POR and results are stored in Raw PCS AON registers. So 
external rtune_req is not needed for normal operation. In normal 
operation, if rtune_req is triggered, then customer will need to copy the 
results in Raw PCS AON registers.

4.1 PHY
The DesignWare Cores Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR PHY, also referred to in this document as raw PCS, 
consists of two hard macro (TX and RX)—Physical Medium Attachment Sublayer (PMA)—and a soft macro, 
referred to as Raw PCS soft macro.
The following sections describe the Raw PCS soft macro, the PMA, and their sub-blocks.
Figure 4-1 shows the PHY top-level architecture, including RX_TOP, TX_TOP, and raw PCS.

4.3.1 Receiver
Figure 4-5 provides the block diagram of the receiver (RX).
Figure 4-5 RX Block Diagram
The receiver functions as follows:
1. The PMA receives differential serial data from a pair of external pads. Receiver characteristics, such as 
equalization and termination, are user-controllable.
2. A continuous-time linear equa lizer (CTLE) is first used to compensate for channel loss.
3. A Slicer is implemented after the CTLE to sample the serial data.
4. The deserializer produces pa rallel data and a parallel data clock for the relevant lane.
4.3.1.1 RX Analog Front End
The analog front end (AFE) circuitry receives off-chip data and boosts high-frequency components to 
compensate part of the channel loss with programmable or adaptive CTLE.
Figure 4-6 RX AFE Diagram
As shown in Figure 4-6, the AFE consists of the following blocks:
■ Termination (TERM)
The TERM block is tuned by the PHY using an off-chip reference resistor.
To configure the RX termination in pull-up or floating mode (see Figure 4-7), use rxX_afe_term_ctrl_-
fix_vp:

Figure 4-7 RX Termination
■ Continuous-time linear equalizer (CTLE)
The CTLE block provides high-frequency boost range from 0–8 dB. The CTLE block has program-
mable boost level and boost bandwidth (BW) control (provided by rxX_afe_ctle_boost and rxX_afe_ct-
le_rtrim, respectively).
To achieve optimal link performance, the receiver equalization parameters are automatically tuned to their 
optimal values for protocols that are adapted.
4.3.1.2 RX Slicer and Deserializer
The Slicer and Deserializer convert serial data from the rx_afe and send the data to the raw PCS as parallel 
data. The Slicer samples the serial data stream, converting it to the 4UI data domain. The Deserializer then 
down-converts the data further, before sending the parallel data to the receiver's PCS channel.
4.3.2 Transmitter
The Transmitter functions as follows:
1. The PMA receives parallel data from  the relevant lane of the raw PCS.
2. The data is serialized and sent to the TX driver, which is a high-speed, current mode, differential-output 
driver. The TX driver characteristics, such as amplitude, pre-emphasis, and termination, are 
user-controllable. 
3. The TX driver output is sent  to a pair of external pads.
Note The PHY supports DC coupled link into RX floating termination.

4.3.2.1 TX Serializer
Parallel data from the raw PCS is latched-in using a clock that is synchronous to the lane's TX clock, and 
then serialized by the Parallel-to-Serial (P2S) converter.
4.3.2.2 TX Termination
The transmit driver is a current-mode driver, with nominally 50 ohm termination on both the TXP and TXN 
outputs, as shown in Figure 4-8. The termination is bandwidth extended through on-die t-coils and output 
matched to target return-loss specifications.
Figure 4-8 TX Termination
4.3.2.3 TX Amplitude
The transmit driver is a current-mode driver, and the DC launch amplitude is determined by the current 
through the main tap. You can control the amplitude by adjusting the C0 tap weight.
Note The PHY supports DC coupled link into far end floating termination.

4.3.2.4 TX Equalization
The TX Digital implements a 3-tap feed-forward equalizer (FFE) with one main tap (C0), one post-cursor tap 
(C+1) and one pre-cursor taps (C-1) as shown in Figure 4-9. These tap values are controlled by 
tx_eq_{pre/main/post} primary input signals.
Figure 4-9 TX Driver Architecture
Where:
■ C0, C-1, and C1 are TX driver main tap, pre-cursor tap and post-cursor tap coefficients
■ C-1 and C1 are defined as negative values
The FFE coefficients C0, C-1, C1 are set by three control signals (tx_eq_main, tx_eq_pre, and tx_eq_post) by 
the following equations:
■ C0 = tx_eq_main
■ |C-1| = tx_eq_pre
■ |C1| = tx_eq_post
TX Equalization with pre-amplitude, post-amplitude, and DC swing is illustrated in Figure 4-10 on page 76.

Figure 4-10 TX Equalization
To calculate the pre-cursor and post-cursor equalization, use the following equations:
■ PRE_EQ_dB    = 20*LOG10((C0 - C-1 - C1) / (C0 + C-1 - C1))
■ POST_EQ_dB = 20*LOG10((C0 - C-1 - C1) / (C0 - C-1 + C1))
4.3.3 Support Module
The PHY includes two support blocks, one for TX and one for RX, working independently.
The support block (see Figure 4-1 on page 67) provides the common functions for:
■ TX and RX clock generation
■ TX and RX termination calibration
■ Biasing voltage
■ Reference clock buffering
4.3.3.1 Reference Clock Input
The PHY requires a reference clock. It can be provided by one of the following sources:
■ External off-chip reference clocks using ref_pad_clk_p and ref_pad_clk_m 
■ On-chip reference clocks using ref_alt_clk_p and ref_alt_clk_m
The external or core-side input is selected by refa_clk_sel. When refa_clk_sel is set to 1 is asserted, the 
external reference clock is used; otherwise, the on-chip reference source is used.
For more information about implementation, see “Reference Clock Implementation” on page 165.
4.3.3.2 Reference Clock Output
The support block includes a reference clock repeat function. A Current-Mode Logic (CML) differential 
reference clock is driven on ref_repeat_clk_{p,m} when ref_repeat_clk_en is asserted. This clock can be used 
as the reference clock source for other PHYs. 
For more information about reference clock output, see “Repeat Clock Specification” on page 168.

4.3.3.3 PLL
Each Support module has a PLL that generate the high-frequency clocks used by the transmitter lane (TX) 
and the receiver (RX). 
The PLL has the following key features: 
■ PLL has a frequency range of 7 GHz - 14 GHz
■ With a 14 GHz clock and quarter-rate clocking, a symbol rate of 56 Gbps is achieved
■ With a 7 GHz clock and quarter-rate clocking, a symbol rate of 28 Gbps is achieved
■ With a 7 GHz clock and 4x oversampling, a symbol rate of 1.75 Gbps is achieved
■ The PLL is an integer PLL, and the simultaneously supported data rates are a function of supplied 
reference clock frequency
4.4 Microcontroller
The raw PCS contains a microcontroller to assist with execution of the raw PCS initialization, configuration, 
and power-state transitions.
The microcontroller firmware uses the APB interface to read and write the raw PCS /PMA internal 
registers. It requires two external memories (not included in the raw PCS).
The external memories connect to the raw PCS through the top-level microcontroller memory interface, see 
“Control Register and Memory Interface” on page 99.
4.4.1 Features
The microcontroller includes the following features:
■ 32-bit Harvard architecture core to execute the firmware
■ Maximum CPU clock frequency is 100 MHz 
■ CPU JTAG maximum clock frequency 100 MHz
■ ICCM instruction closely-coupled memory populated to 64 kB (must be implemented externally)
■ DCCM data closely-coupled memory populated to 64 kB (must be implemented externally)
■ An IO port with APB interface mapping raw PCS/PMA Registers into microcontroller memory space
■ A pair of AHB-Lite interfaces for ICCM and DCCM loading and for general access for debug purposes
■ Big Endian memory orientation
■ JTAG debug unit for runtime firmware debugging
■ Real time counter to implement firmware delay
■ Interrupt controller to handle critical events
■ Watchdog timer to protect the system from CPU core and firmware faults

5.1 PHY Supply Power-Up
Power up the vp* and vph power supplies and then proceed with the PHY reset and initialization sequence 
(vp* indicates the grouping of all core supplies as well as vp and vpdig).
For a complete power supply pin list, including analog and digital grounds, see “Power Supply Signals” on 
page 64.
5.2 Raw PCS Initia lization Sequence
You can initialize the raw PCS using a hardware or firmware based option. This impacts many of the raw 
PCS configuration settings. In the following sections, make sure you follow the procedures according to the 
raw PCS initialization option you choose (hardware or firmware based).
The hardware based solution should be only used during the initial phase of the PHY model integration, 
and for debug purposes. The PHY initialization must be done by the Firmware. The Raw PCS has a built-in 
microcontroller core that needs to be initialized. Once the microcontroller core boots up, the IP Firmware 
helps with the initialization sequence based on commands described in “Firmware Based” on page 81.
5.2.1 Hardware Based
The hardware based test setup, and initialization must be done during the debug, and/or initial phase of 
the PHY model integration.
This mode gets deprecated by the usage of the Firmware mode (see “Firmware Based” on page 81), that is 
the default mode of usage of the PHY. 
Figure 5-1 shows the PHY hardware based initialization sequence. Note that the hardware based sequence 
cannot see full functional PIPE interface behavior.
Figure 5-1 Hardware Based PHY Initialization Sequence
Note ■ The PHY does not support power modes. The PHY is either On or Off.
■ The state of the PHY is unknown after power up but before assertion of reset.
■ While the unknown state is undesirable, the PHY has been designed to prevent damage if 
it is left in this state. 
■ Supply ramp should be monotonic with a ramp time of no faster than 10 us and a rate no 
faster than (supply voltage)/10 µs.
■ vpdig ramping up before vp is supported; vp ramping down before vpdig is supported.
■ vp ramping up before vpdig is not supported; vpdig ramping down before vp is not 
supported.
■ vpdig and vp ramping up/down together is supported.

To initialize or reset the raw PCS through hardware, follow the next steps:
1. Power-up power supplies and make sure they are stable.
2. Assert resets.
3. De-assert the resets. PHY is ready for APB programming.
4. Program PHY register to specify the bring up mode as hardware based (current programing is done on 
the provided testbench).
5. Program the PHY registers for the desired co nfiguration, such as data rate (see “Datarates” on page 90).
5.2.2 Firmware Based
Figure 5-2 shows the PHY firmware based initialization sequence.
Figure 5-2 Firmware Based PHY Initialization Sequence
To initialize or reset the raw PCS through firmware, follow the next steps:
1. Power-up power supplies and make sure they are stable.
2. Assert resets.
Resets need to be asserted for a minimum of 25 us after power supplies are ramped-up (power-on). For 
any subsequent reset assertions (warm-reset) the resets need to be asserted for a minimum of 10 ns.
3. De-assert the resets.
 Ensure the APB clock and CPU clocks are stable. Raw PCS is now ready for APB programming.
4. Load the ICCM SRAM with th e provided firmware image using any of the available methods.
This can be done through one of the AHB or APB interfaces, see “Control Register and Memory Inter-
face” on page 99.
5. Program RAW_PCS register to run the CPU. Write 
APB::RAW_PCS::CPU_CFG::cpu_run_req=1b1.
The CPU is halted by default upon removal of reset. This step allows the processor to start fetching the 
firmware from the SRAM and executing it.

5.3 PHY Initialization Sequence
This section describes the PIPE lite interface for the D2D connections. Since 112G XSR PHY is a product 
protocol independent, it uses a lite version of the Pipe CxL 5.2.
The PIPE lite interface is handled by firmware.
Figure 5-3 shows an example implementation of the PIPE interface, having near-end and a far-end 
connection, assuming x16 connection PHY, and forwarded clock mode connection. For more information 
about forwarded clock connection, see “Forwarded Clock Mode” on page 93.
Figure 5-3 PIPE Interface PHY and Controller Example
The software layer implements the protocol for communication and handshaking between the far-end and 
near-end sides of the PHY. Communication is done through the controller, each side of the PHY has a 
controller. The handshaking uses sideband communication.
Although the controller side supports several links (on the example there are four), you only need to 
perform one synthesis, to respect the clock tree distribution.
The PHY interface uses only one clock and one data stream.
The startup sequence depends on the selected clock mode configuration. This mode is selected prior to the 
initialization.

side (four in the next example) only one synthesis must be done to respect the clock tree distribution.
For PHY interface, you need to have one clock and one data stream.

89
5.4 Datapath 
TX and RX datapaths are activated after PIPE Lite interface is initialized. For details, see “PHY Initialization 
Sequence” on page 83. 
5.4.1 TX Datapath
Make sure that the pipe_tx_clk is synchronous with the full bus of data on the pipe_tx_data[N_lanes*64-1:0] 
for the TX datapath data symbols start to be captured by the PHY on the rising edge of pipe_tx_clk, when 
pipe_phy_status[N_Lane] is de-asserted.
For lanes belonging to the same link ensure that the pipe_tx_data_ is provided from the same link using the 
pipe_tx_clk.
The PMA contains a circuit that aligns the incoming txX_clk with the internal transmit clock used in the 
Analog. This alignment is done internally by the Firmware on the calibrations phase. 
This alignment results in a maximum TX skew on txX_p/m across different lanes in the same link to within 
2 unit-intervals (UI) + 500 ps. 
This procedure requires (mandatory) that Lane 0 is always On.
5.4.2 RX Datapath
The RX datapath data symbols are valid and outputted on the rising edge of the pipe_rx_clk, when 
pipe_rx_valid[N_lane] is set to one. 
Each lane of the PHY has a correspondent pipe_status and rx_valid signal, meaning that lanes are being 
powered up individually. 
The PMA contains a circuit that aligns the internal clock of each lane to the top clock This alignment is done 
internally by the Firmware on the calibrations phase. 
This procedure requires (mandatory) that Lane 0 is always On.
5.4.3 Datapath Latency
The TX and RX datapath latencies for the PMA are measured as per Figure 5-5 and Figure 5-6. 
On TX there is a clock alignment mechanism in the PHY to align the internal transmit clocks of each lane to 
the common pipe_tx_clk inputs. This alignment results in a maximum TX skew on txX_p and txX_m across 
different lanes of 500 ps.
Figure 5-5 Transmit Datapath Latency Measurement

5.5 Datarates 
The TX and RX datapaths support different datarates. The datarate configuration affects both TX and RX 
datatpaths.
TX and RX do not support different datarate configurations, rate needs to always be aligned. 
The data rate pin rate[2:0] only affects the amount of data per clock cycle. Changing the rate does not 
affect either the PLL frequencies, or the pipe_tx/rx_clk frequency.
The clock frequency of the PHY is only affected by the sup_cntx_sel information, for more information 
see Appendix A, “PHY Configuration”. 
Table 5-5 shows the PHY operation datarates.
RX [UI] RX [ns] TX [UI] TX [ns] Total [UI] Total [ns]
Minimum 164 2.93 146 2.62 310 5.55
M a x i m u m N AN A N AN A N A N A
Note Latency numbers are under review. 
Attention
Rate signal cannot be changed dynamically. The PHY does not support rate changes on 
demand. 
To change the rate of the PHY, you need to restart the PHY by asserting and resets and 
re-doing the power-up sequences. Not doing this may result in compromised data.

5.6 Clock Modes 
The PHY supports the following clock modes configurations. 
■ forwarded clock
■ common clock
■ embedded clock
Use pin clk_mode_i[2:0] to set the clock mode. You can set the clock source and the type of 
configuration. This pin must be set before startup.
Table 5-6 is coded in the firmware, no other configuration is needed for control the clock mode.
See Appendix A, “PHY Configuration” for more information about the PHY configuration.

5.7 Control Register and Memory Interface
The raw PCS has two control register interfaces and three memory interfaces.
The control register interfaces are used for register access and for the CPU SRAMs slow access; they are 
listed as follows:
■ APB external interface, is the main interface for accessing raw PCS registers. It can also be used to 
access ICCM and DCCM memories.
■ Raw PCS JTAG interface, is an alternative interface to the APB, it has the same access capability.
The memory interfaces are listed as follows:
■ CCM AHB-Lite interface, provides a fast loading and access to the CPU SRAMs.
■ SRAM interfaces:
❑ ICCM interface, used by the CPU to access the external instruction memory.
❑ DCCM interface, used by the CPU to access the external data memory.
Figure 5-11 shows the raw PCS control register and memory interfaces.
Figure 5-11 Raw PCS Control Register and Memory Interfaces

5.7.1.1 RiW Override Value Registers
A register field of type RiW is a Read-internal/Write type register.
The RiW register type is used for enabling silicon debug and software workaround by overriding or 
observing the internal signal value. 
Figure 5-13 shows the structure of the override mechanism using this RiW register.
Figure 5-13 Override Operation 
The value returned on reading a RiW register depends on the status of override enable register. If the 
override is enabled, the read gives the value of the override value register. If the override is not enabled, the 
read gives the value of the internal signal.
5.7.2 APB Interface Signaling
The raw PCS pins under APB Interface class can be used to configure the PHY registers. See “APB Interface 
Signals” on page 41 for more information about these pins.
The APB interface is compatible with APB4—for details about the APB protocol, see the AMBA APB 
Protocol Specification v2.0—with the exception of no t supporting the PPROT protection signal. For more 
information about these pins, see “APB Interface Signals” on page 41.
The following figures show timing diagram of APB Interface transactions.
■ APB read and write takes several APB clock cycles to complete. See “APB Worst Case Access Latency” 
for worst case latency. 
■ pstrb_i can be used to do a byte level write transaction. For the read transaction, pstrb_i status is 
ignored.
Note RiW register type applies to all registers that have the Volatile attribute set to true in the 
description.

Figure 5-14 shows the APB interface timing diagram for APB write transaction.
Figure 5-14  APB Write Transaction Timing Diagram
1. Transaction 1 
 Writes all 32 bits of D0 into address A0. pready_o asserts after several clock cycles to indicate the 
completion of the transfer.
2. Transaction 2
The next write transfer into address A1 follows without de-asserting pselx_i. In this case, 
penable_i must be de-asserted for only one clock cycle. 
3. Transaction 3 
Transfer into address A2 is similar to the first one with the exception of only the least significant byte 
of D2 is written into A2. pready_o is valid for only one APB clock cycle, once asserted. pslverr_o 
is valid when pready_o is asserted; it is valid for only one clock cycle.
pclk_i
paddr_i[17:0] A0 A1 A2A0 A1 A2
pwdata_i[31:0] D0 D1 D2D0 D1 D2
pselx_i

■ Accessing unused address space (for the unused addresses see Figure 5-12)
■ Attempting to access ICCM while internal bootloader is running and not done

Requirements to use the CCM AHB-Lite Interface:
■ The CPU clock must be running (cpu_clk_i)
■ The PHY reset must be de-asserted (rstn_i)
■ APB::RAW_PCS::RAW_PCS_CFG::ccm_fast_access_sel=0b1
The CCM AHB-Lite Interface can be used to Read the CCMs while the CPU is halted 
(cpu_sys_halt_o = 1’b1) or running (cpu_sys_halt_o = 1’b0). Make sure that while loading the 
firmware into the ICCM, the CPU is halted (cpu_sys_halt_o = 1’b1). The CCM AHB-Lite Interface 
supports AHB Lite burst transfers and, if used, it can sustain one data transfer every clock cycle. According 
to AHB-Lite protocol, burst size must not exceed 1 kB Address boundary. A single non-burst transfer can 
take 3 to 20 clock cycles depending on whether the CPU core is running (cpu_sys_halt_o = 1’b0) or 
not, and also on the value of ccm_ahb_prio_i  which sets the priority of the access. The following are 
example timing diagrams, for more details, refer to AHB-Lite protocol specification

To load 64 kB of firmware, use 64 bursts of 1 kB of burst size. 
By keeping ccm_ahb_prio_i asserted, you guarantee that this bus is given a high priority and 
avoid having extra cycles. This signal is not part of the AHB protocol.

DCCM is accessed; it is required to retain its base address in the address bus until the data transfer phase is 
complete. 
As a requirement of the raw PCS implementation, ICCM and DCCM accesses must not be pipelined 
together. In general, pipelining is only supported for multiple accesses to the same memory (ICCM or 
DCCM). The base address must remain the same until the data phase is complete. This requirement is 
applicable to both burst and single transfers. 
5.7.3.1 SRAM Interface
The microcontroller uses two closely coupled SRAM arrays called DCCM and ICCM for storage of program 
data and instructions. The microcontroller accesses the DCCM and ICCM simultaneously over separate 
interfaces. To support the firmware, the array sizes must be 16K x 32b (64 kB) for ICCM array and 16K x 32b 
(64 kB) for the DCCM array.
In order to optimize firmware spacing, the firmware is separated into two files:
■ firmware file for the ICCM, *_iccm.fw, should be loaded into the ICCM;
■ firmware file for the DCCM, *_dccm.fw, should be loaded into the DCCM.
To ensure timing closure and to minimize area required for SRAM, it is not recommended for the 
microcontroller SRAM to include ECC. The following is the recommended procedure during firmware 
operation when an extremely heightened level of SRAM fidelity is required: 
1. Write firmware into SRAM. 
2. Read firmware from SRAM and compare to ensure it is correctly programmed. 
3. Execute Firmware. 
4. Read Firmware from SRAM to compare agai n to ensure it is correctly programmed.

5. Proceed with operation. 
For almost all systems, this is not expected to be required.
For the purposes of timing closure, the microcontroller SRAMs should be hardened together with the raw 
PCS, or abutted directly up against the hardened raw PCS. For best results, it is recommended hardening 
the SRAMs with the raw PCS. This allows for best placement and timing closure. When reviewing initial 
SRAM choices, a Clock-to-Q of less than one-third of the CPU clock cycle period is a recommended starting 
point. As the physical design development progresses, a better understanding of overall device 
performance and timing is obtained. Additional trade-offs may be made in the SRAM selection to best suit 
your system, while still meeting overall SRAM to raw PCS timing requirements.
The two memory arrays must be byte-writeable, fully synchronous designs. Each can be implemented as a 
single memory instance, or built from multiple memory instances. The memories must retain their output 
values when chip enable is not asserted. You must add latches if your memories change their outputs to the 
X or Z state when chip enable is not asserted. After address is applied, the raw PCS expects valid data in the 
next clock cycle.
Figure 5-19 Implementation Example of 64 kB ICCM
The microcontroller design is provided with a behavioral model of the ICCM and DCCM closely-coupled 
memories. Simulation on the behavioral model can be started immediately before the actual ICCM and 
DCCM arrays are constructed. Using the behavioral model establishes working tests in simulation to build 
confidence for simulations with the memory-based array design.
Figure 5-21 and Figure 5-22 show the write and read timing diagrams for the ICCM and DCCM SRAMs

5.7.4 Raw PCS JTAG Interface
The RAWPCS JTAG interface provides access to an internal TAP controller which implements the APBSEL 
instruction used for writing and reading registers in the PHY. It can also be used to read/write SRAMs if 
APB::RAW_PCS::RAW_PCS_CFG::ccm_fast_access_sel=0b0, CPU clock is running and PHY reset is 
de-asserted. The JTAG interface is enabled when input jtag_apb_sel_i is asserted to 1. There is one 
JTAG interface per PHY; for configurations where there are multiple PHYs aggregated (instantiated), there 
is more than one JTAG interface. To control these with a single TAP controller, the JTAG interfaces can be 
daisy chained externally. For more information about the Raw PCS JTAG Interface signals see DFT JTAG 
Signals.

shifted into jtag_tdi_i input.

5.7.5 CPU JTAG Interface
The CPU JTAG Interface provides a debugger interface for the CPU withing the Raw PCS. This section 
describes how the CPU JTAG is used to control the processor and to access various resources: 
■ Processor-internal resources:
❑ Core Registers
❑ Auxiliary Registers
❑ ICCM and DCCM Closely coupled memories 
■ Processor-visible external resources, peripherals (PHY Registers)
For more information about the CPU JTAG signals see “CPU Signals” on page 46. The JTAG module 
contains logic for communicating with the microcontroller processor and its memory system. The host 
interacts with this module through high-level commands that specify transaction parameters. The module 
supports the IEEE 1149.1 four-wire JTAG interface.
5.7.5.1 JTAG 1149.1 Port
Figure 5-23 shows how the JTAG communications port integrates within the microcontroller system. The 
port is linked to the host interface of the microcontroller processor and to system memory through the 
data-memory pipeline. The JTAG port is also known as TAP (Test Access Port).
Figure 5-23 JTAG 1149.1 Port with Microcontroller Processor

The JTAG module includes the internal registers as shown in Table 5-10. The host can perform a read or 
write transaction to a memory location or a microcontroller register using these internal registers. Six of the

5.7.5.2.1 JTAG 1149.1 Instruction Register
The instruction register is used to access all data registers. Each data register is addressed by a unique 4-bit 
instruction code, see Figure 5-24.
Figure 5-24 Instruction Code
The correct code must be written into the instruction register to access the required data register. The 
instruction shift register is loaded with 0x1 in capture-IR, so that external circuitry—by looking for a 
transition when in shift-IR—can detect  a stuck-at fault in the JTAG chain.
Because of this behavior, which is specified in IEEE 1149.1, the current contents of the instruction register 
cannot be read by the external circuitry. The register itself is initialized to point to the IDCODE register in 
test-logic-reset.
In addition to accessing the data registers, the instruction register is also used to select a test sequence to 
apply to the device.
5.7.5.2.2 JTAG 1149.1 Status Register (Instruction Code 0x8)
The JTAG status register is read-only and is used by the host device to obtain important information on the 
state of the JTAG module or the result of debug operations. See Figure 5-25 for the register bit mapping.

Each field in the JTAG status register reflects the following information:
■ Bit 0 - The stalled (ST) flag indicates that the current transaction is still in progress.
■ Bit 1 - When true, the failure (FL) flag indicates that a read (or write) has failed. For example, this flag 
is set if an access to an unimplemented core occurs. The failure flag is cleared automatically when a 
new transaction starts.
■ Bit 2 - The ready (RD) flag indicates whether the JTAG module is available to accept another transac-
tion command. This flag is set when a transaction has just completed or when the JTAG module is idle.
■ Bit 3 - Always zero.
■ Bit 4 - The run (RU) flag is set to one when the microcontroller processor is running. This bit is cleared 
to zero when the processor is halted. This flag is the inverse of the halt bit in the microcontroller 
STATUS32 register.
■ Bit 5 - The Reset Applied (RA) flag is used by the debug host to determine that a target reset has 
occurred. The RA flag is set to 1 on a hard reset of the CPU and can be written only by the host.
■ Bits[31:6] - Reserved.
5.7.5.2.3 JTAG 1149.1 Transaction-Command Register (Instruction Code 0x9)
The transaction-command register is used to specify the communication transaction to be performed.

Figure 5-27 Instruction Code 0xA
Accesses to memory must always be given in bytes. Access to the microcontroller internal registers is 
specified by their register numbers. The value contained in this register is automatically incremented by 
four (a memory access) or one (a register access) when a read or write transaction completes. This feature is 
used to save cycle time when downloading or uploading a stream of data. For this reason, the register does 
not need to be rewritten with the next address value.
5.7.5.2.5 JTAG 1149.1 Data Register (Instruction Code 0xB)
The data register performs two functions. When data is written to this register, it is placed into a write 
buffer that drives two write data buses, one for the microcontroller host interface and another for the 
memory arbitrator interface. This bus is used to specify the data contents to be written when performing a 
write transaction.
Figure 5-28 Instruction Code 0xB
A read buffer is selected to read this register. The read buffer stores data retrieved from the target device 
during a read transaction. The appropriate read data bus (arbitrator or host interface) is selected according 
to which device the host is accessing.
5.7.5.2.6 JTAG 1149.1 IDCODE register (Instruction Code 0xC)
The IDCODE register is used by the JTAG emulator to identify the core. The IDCODE register is selected 
when a reset is applied to the JTAG module. 
Figure 5-29 Instruction Code 0xC
Each field in the IDCODE register reflects the following information:
■ Bits[31:28] – This field is set to 0x1.
■ Bits[27:18] – This field is set to 0x0.
■ Bits[17:12] – This field is set to 0x04.
■ Bits[11:1] – This field is set to 0x258.
■ Bit 0 – This field is fixed at 1, as specified in the IEEE 1149.1-2001 standard. This field is used with 
Bits[11:1] to allow automatic discovery when the chain is initialized.
JEDEC never assigns the manufacturer's code 0x7F in group zero. The JTAG emulator can therefore 
shift the 32-bit IDCODE 0x000000FF into TDI at the beginning of the chain after reset. The standard

specifies that upon receiving a TCK when in the reset-test-logic state, the instruction register must be 
initialized to point to the IDCODE register if this register exists, and to the BYPASS register otherwise.
In capture-DR, the shift register is loaded with this 32-bit code if IDCODE is in the instruction register, 
and with a single bit of zero if BYPASS is. Thus the external circuitry can examine TDO in shift-DR and 
determine whether this TAP controller has only a one-bit bypass register (TDO = zero), or a 32-bit 
IDCODE register (TDO = 1). By shifting through and examining until 0x000000FF appears, the 
emulator can uniquely identify the number and kind of devices in the chain.
5.7.5.2.7 JTAG 1149.1 Bypass Register (Instruction Code 0xF)
The bypass register is required as part of the IEEE STD 1149.1 standard.
Figure 5-30 Instruction Code 0xF
When this register is selected, the serial data input (TDI) is connected to the serial data output (TDO) 
through this register. The data on TDI is passed to TDO on the rising edge of the JTAG clock TCK when in 
shift-DR, and the register is initialized to 0 in capture-DR. In all other states, the data in this register is held.
5.7.5.3 JTAG 1149.1 TAP Controller
The Test Access Port (TAP) controller is central to the operation of the JTAG module as shown in 
Figure 5-31. All internal register accesses are performed serially using the TAP controller. An accompanying 
block, the debug port, performs the majority of internal tasks (accessing internal JTAG registers) and 
external tasks. The debug port and the TAP controller are clocked by TCK.
On the other side of the BVCI Debug Interface, the CPU debug module contains the address, data, 
command, and status registers, and handles the host interface and data memory pipeline transactions. This 
allows you to optionally replace the JTAG port with a custom interface to an external debugger or to 
another processor in a master-slave configuration.

The TAP controller is an internal state machine that is controlled entirely by the host using the TMS and 
TCK interface signals. The controller is used to indirectly initiate communication transactions and access the 
internal JTAG registers.
5.8 TX and RX Power States
The PHY does not support power states. It only supports:
■ Power Down (OFF)
■ Mission Mode (ON)

5.10 Firmware
The PHY can have all configurations available over IP Firmware. This reduces the level of complexity when configuring the PHY, including registers 
configurations, startup, change clock modes, change clock frequencies, and when performing all the needed calibrations to have the PHY in mission 
mode. Firmware is also responsible to handle the PIPE interface.
Before launching the Firmware make sure that the primary inputs are set to the desired configuration values. Reference clocks are set according to 
these configurations.
■ PHY data rate: pin rate[1:0]
■ Clock rates (PLL Frequencies): pin sup_cntx_sel see Appendix A, “PHY Configuration”
■ signal_type (PAM4/NRZ)
■ Clock modes: pin clk_mode[2:0]see Table 5-6 on page 93

Test and Diagnostics

===== PAGE 118 =====
Synopsys, Inc. 
Test and Diagnostics Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR x8 PHY for TSMC 12-nm FFC/1.8V Databook
SolvNetPlus

May 17, 2022

6.1 Test Modes
This section provides information about test modes.
The PHY supports the following test modes:
■ Scan Mode
■ IDDQ
■ Loopback Modes
6.1.1 Scan Mode (S tuck-at, At-speed)
The PHY supports stuck-at and at-speed scan for its digital logic. 
Set scan_mode =1’b1 to enter scan mode.
When entering scan mode, data in the analog interface is internally gated.
To achieve coverage of the metrics below, all other inputs must be controllable and all outputs must be 
observable.
The following are scan features requirements in the PHY:
■ The PHY provides independent scan clock inputs per internal clock domain.
■ Primary clock inputs to the PHY must have scan clocks supplied to them externally in scan mode.
■ All clock outputs from the PHY, except ref_repeat_clk_p/m, have a scan clock input muxed on them.
■ Scan clocks must have 50% duty cycle.
■ Synchronous inputs and outputs are at-speed scannable.
■ All resets (primary input resets or internally generated resets) are over-ridden to primary scan reset.
■ Input scan_set_rst in scan mode.
■ Maximum scan shift frequency is 100 MHz.
■ Maximum scan chain length is 350 flops.
■ All scan chains are positive-edge triggered.
■ All scan chains are terminated with lockup latches.
■ All analog blocks are powered down in scan mode. To prevent propagation of logic X's in the scan 
chain, the outputs of all the analog blocks feeding the digital scannable logic are over-ridden to known 
states.
■ Coverage of the soft-macro (RAW PCS) is place-and-route implementation dependent.
■ Stuck-At-Fault (SAF) coverage of the PMA is greater than 97%.
Note PMA .lib ETM modes:
■ NORMAL—For Functional mode
■ SCAN—For Stuck-at Shift and Capture mode
In this mode, Shift and Capture happens at Scan Clock Frequency.
■ SCAN_ASST—For At Sp eed Scan Testing
In this mode, Shift occurs at Scan Clock Frequency, whereas Capture occurs at 
Functional mode frequency.
===== PAGE 119 =====
Synopsys, Inc. 
Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR x8 PHY for TSMC 12-nm FFC/1.8V Databook                                                         Test and Diagnostics
SolvNetPlus 
DesignWare
PHY Version: 2.02a 
May 17, 2022
121
■ Transition-Delay-Fault (TDF) coverage of the PMA is greater than 85%.
The scan clocks in Table 8-9 on page 152 support a maximum shift frequency of 100 MHz. Stuck-at and 
At-speed is done according to Table 8-9 on page 152.
6.1.2 IDDQ Mode
In IDDQ test mode, all the analog blocks are powered down to allow leakage measurement.
Since the PHY has different PMA’s, the IDDQ test can be done independently per PMA.
IDDQ current consumption measures are done at the end of the sequence, with the clock stopped.
6.1.2.1 RX IDDQ Test Sequence
Follow these steps to perform IDDQ measurements on the RX.
1. Set all supplies on.
2. Set all PHY clocks off, except apb.
3. Do apb reset toggle:
a. presetn_i set to 0;
b. presetn_i set to 1.
4. Do the following apb writes:
a. rx_sup.reg_sup_ana_ovrdval_9_h.ana_refa_ana_clk_en_i.set(1'b0);
b. rx_sup.reg_sup_ana_ovrdval_9_h.ana_ref_ro_ana_clk_en_i.set(1'b0);
c. rx_sup.reg_sup_ana_ovrdval_9_h.ana _ref_ana_diffbuf_clk_en_i.set(1'b0).
5. Set apb clocks off.
6. Set the following reset signals to 0:
a. raw_pcs_rstn_i;
b. rx_rstn_i;
c. presetn_i.
7. Wait 2us.
8. Carry out the measurement.
6.1.2.2 TX IDDQ Test Sequence
Follow these steps to perform IDDQ measurements on the TX.
1. Set all supplies on.
2. Set all PHY clocks off, except apb.
3. Do apb reset toggle:
a. presetn_i set to 0;
b. presetn_i set to 1.
===== PAGE 120 =====
Synopsys, Inc. 
Test and Diagnostics Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR x8 PHY for TSMC 12-nm FFC/1.8V Databook
SolvNetPlus 
DesignWare
PHY Version: 2.02a 
May 17, 2022
122
4. Do the following apb writes:
a. tx_sup.reg_sup_ana_ovrdval_9_h.ana_refa_ana_clk_en_i.set(1'b0);
b. tx_sup.reg_sup_ana_ovrdval_9_h.ana_ref_ro_ana_clk_en_i.set(1'b0);
c. tx_sup.reg_sup_ana_ovrdval_9_h.ana_ref_ana_diffbuf_clk_en_i.set(1'b0).
5. Set apb clocks off.
6. Set the following reset signals to 0:
a. raw_pcs_rstn_i;
b. tx_rstn_i;
c. presetn_i.
7. Wait 2us.
8. Carry out the measurement.

6.2 Diagnostics
The PHY provides the following diagnostic features to enhance testbench characterization and ATE testing:
■ Loopback functions
❑ parallel loopback at the raw_pcs level
❑ per-lane TX serial internal loopback
❑ per-lane RX serial internal loopback
■ BERT (independent per-lane)
❑ 7th, 15th, and 31st order polynomial pattern generation and recognition
❑ 10th pseudo random quaternary sequence pattern generation for PAM4
❑ Generation of simple custom test patterns
❑ Error insertion and detection
■ TX non-destructive internal eye monitor (TX driver output)
■ RX non-destructive internal eye monitor (RX AFE output)
■ Analog Test Bus to monitor internal analog signals
■ Digital monitor for internal digital clocks
6.2.1 Loopback Modes
The Raw PCS supports loopback modes on the serial, and parallel sides.
The following are on-die and die-to-die loopback modes (described in the subsequent sections):
■ Raw PCS level loopback
■ Internal Per-lane loopback TX and RX individually
■ Die-to-die loopback

Figure 6-1 Loopback Modes
6.2.1.1 Raw PCS Level Loopback
This loopback is also called near-end parallel loopback, or Shallow Loopback (marked (a) in Figure 6-1).
In the raw PCS level loopback mode the TX parallel input clock and data at the raw PCS input side are 
looped-back connected to RX parallel output clock and data. 
To enable the shallow loopback mode, use the next register setting: 
APB::RAW_PCS::tx_to_rx_lpbk_en:: =1’b1

===== PAGE 123 =====
Synopsys, Inc. 
Die-to-Die 112G USR/XSR x8 PHY for TSMC 12-nm FFC/1.8V Databook                                                         Test and Diagnostics
SolvNetPlus 
DesignWare

May 17, 2022

6.2.1.2 Internal Per-Lane Loopback
This loopback is done per lane inner the lane RX or TX independently (marked (b) in Figure 6-1 on page 
124).
This mode uses the built in PRBS Generator to generate data on the digital, sends it to ANA, which sends 
back to DIG to be matched on the Pattern matcher. Using this mode, the NRZ Datapath between analog and 
digital can be verified.
This mode requires an additional calibration to be done, by asserting the following registers the calibration 
is executed by the firmware. 
Since the TX and RX are two different PMA’s there is no need to calibrate both if not desired. 
6.2.1.3 Die-to-Die Loopback
The die-to-die loopback (marked (c) in Figure 6-1 on page 124) validates the data communication between 
two dies. This mode uses a near-end XSR and a far-end XSR. Loopback is programed so that the near-end 
TX activates the PRSBS Pattern Gen. 
Data is sent through the TX to the opposite RX on the far-end, where it is sent back through the far-end die’s 
TX, received by near-end die’s RX, and then pattern matched. 
Note This loopback mode is only supported on NRZ mode. PAM4 is not supported.
Note For more information, refer to the Application Note for ATE testcases.

7.1 Overview
The product deliverables include a Verilog RTL for the raw PCS soft macro portion and Verilog behavioral 
models of the PMAs. Contrary to the Verilog RTL for the soft macro, which can be synthesized into a 
gate-level netlist by a synthesis tool, the Verilog behavioral models of the PMAs include a netlist of generic 
GTECH gates and a non-synthesizable behavioral model of the analog circuits.
In the set of deliverables, the raw PCS Verilog RTL and the PMAs Verilog behavioral models reside at:
■ Raw PCS: ./Latest/macro/rtl
■ PMA TX Verilog model: ./Latest/pma_tx/behavior
■ PMA RX Verilog model: ./Latest/pma_rx/behavior
Together, they form the Verilog model of the PHY. The PHY model allows you to run functional verification 
of your logic along with the PHY.
7.2 Verilog Function
The Verilog model uses a four-state (0, 1, X, Z) simulator. Features that require analog voltage and current 
values transmitted over wires cannot be simulated with this model.
The following features are not accurately modeled by the PHY model:
■ Receive and transmit I/O terminations.
■ Termination resistor tuning and measurement.
■ Analog test buses.
■ Transmitter equalization settings (pre-cursor, swing, and post-cursor) on the transmit serial lines, 
TXP/N[<n>].
■ Receiver equalizer settings.
Note This PHY model and testbench do not represent an example of exhaustive PHY testing.

8.1 Floorplan Considerations
When placing the assembled PMA keep in mind the next considerations.
8.1.1 PMA Rotation and Mirroring
The POLY gates within the PMA must be vertical according to foundry rules, see Figure 8-1.
The poly gate orientation is indicated by ‘_ns’/’_ew’ suffix in PMA name.
■ ‘_ns’ indicates that the poly gates are oriented vertically or ‘north-south’ in this PMA.
■ ‘_ew’ indicates that the poly gates are oriented horizontally or ‘east-west’ in this PMA.
Depending on the process, all poly gates may need to be oriented in the same direction on a single SoC. 
The PMA may need customization in order to support rotation or mirroring. For more information about 
PMA rotation and mirroring, contact Synopsys support.
All diagrams and physical integration guidelines provided refer to the ‘_ns’ poly orientation of the PMA 
and for process nodes which do NOT permit mixed orientation poly gates on an SoC.
Figure 8-1 Default Gate Orientation
The PMA hard macro is designed to be placed in either north/south or east/west side of the die, as shown 
in Figure 8-2.
Figure 8-2 PMA Rotation and Mirroring
The PMA may be rotated by 180°, but not 90° or 270° due to the vertical POLY requirement.

8.2 Spacing Requirements
8.2.1 IP Components with Themselves
Multiple instances of the PMA can be placed side-by-side on north and south. 
When multiple instances of the PMA are placed adjacent to each other side-by-side on the SoC, the spacing 
requirement is the greater of either:
■ A minimum of 5 μm from the PMA boundary on South, East and West directions
■ Minimum spacing to meet bump-to-bump spacing and bump-pitch rules at SoC level
❑ Sometimes due to design restrictions the bumps can overhang the side of the IP and/or do not 
meet ½ bump spacing rules.
❑ For optimal placement options for multiple instances contact Synopsys Support. 
8.2.2 IP Components and other  Un-related Blocks or Logic 
Synopsys recommends a 5 µm spacing for any core logic instance placement around the IP for all sides.
The north edge of the PHY can include signal routing and power meshes as required for connectivity to the 
PHY.
It is recommended routing and power-grid keep-out of 5 µm for the east, west and south edges of the PMA 
macro for all metal and RDL layers which are included in the PMA macro. These edges should only include 
density layer fill shapes as generated by the foundry fill utility.
There should be no SoC level routing over top of the PMA macro.
If there are bump pads which overhang the edge of the PMA macro the SoC level RDL routing should 
maintain the keep-out from the edge of these bumps
8.2.3 IP Component and Chip Edge
The PMA is designed for optimal placement on the south edge of the die.
To meet the UBM to chip-edge spacing rule the SoC level designer may need to place the PMA with an 
offset from chip edge. SoC level designer must verify if this placement follows the foundry design-rule and 
pad rule verification decks.
8.2.4 Other Placement Notes
8.2.4.1 PMA Fill
The PMA comes pre-filled. When you run density layer fill at the chip level, no fill shapes are to be added 
within the PMA.
8.2.4.2 Density Layer Fill
The PHY contains all necessary BEOL and FEOL fill shapes to meet all density rules unless otherwise 
specified in the package readme.

When the foundry fill utilities are run at SoC level, there should not be any additional fill shapes added over 
the PMA macro.
8.3 Packaging/Pad Information
This section provides information about the bump map, pad size, and associated diagrams.
8.3.1 Flip-Chip 
PMA Bump pitch, bump type and sizing have been designed based on optimized bump placement for the 
IP. The bump pad RDL, passivation opening and UBM sizing are based on foundry required dimensions for 
the PMA bump pitch.
For PMA bump pitch and pin location information for package design refer to the RDL pin locations in the 
LEF delivery file.
For different bump pitch, pad type or pad sizing, contact Synopsys sales manager for customization option.
Note Ensure that the under-bump metalization (UBM) is the correct size. Foundry and 
packaging typically require that the UBM shape used for the bump is the same for all 
bumps within the same die. The size of the UBM shape is dictated by the minimum 
bump pitch in the die.

The bump maps shown in this section are for reference purposes only. For dimensions, 
refer to the .lef in the release package.

8.4 Operating Conditions
This section details the operating conditions of the PMA.
8.4.1 Temperature
Table 8-3 lists the PMA supported operating temperature range.
Table 8-3 PMA Operating Temperature Range 
Temperature drift after initial startup adaptation and calibration can affect the performance of PMA, 
particularly the receiver. The amount of temperature drift that the PMA can withstand before violating the 
protocol bit error rate limit depends on the receiver, the data rate, and external channel impairments. If 
there is a temperature drift, it may be necessary to re-adapt and re-calibrate the receiver. Sensitivity to this 
effect can be reduced by implementing continuous offset correction and adaptation (CCA).
This PHY implements continuous offset calibration and adaptation (CCA). This reduces but does not zero 
out the sensitivity of the receiver to changes in temperature. Changes in temperature may still result in 
reduced eye opening as seen by the receiver and will reduce margin for jitter tolerance.
8.4.2 Electromigration
The electromigration limit of the PMA is 88KPOH@105°C.
Operation Temperature Range Description
Performance -20 to 105°C Meets electrical s pecifications as specified in the databook.
Functional -40 to 125°C
Analog and digital sub-blocks operational but will exhibit reduced 
margin to design targets; overall RX and TX may not meet 
specification at temperature extremes; PHY may negotiate to 
lower data rate or PHY may exhibit higher bit error rate (BER) 
performance.
Note Operating at conditions that exceed the electromigration limits of PMA reduces the lifetime of 
the PMA. While the PMA can meet performance requirements at higher temperature, this is 
expected to be a short term condition, such as temporary failure in cooling and so on.

8.5 Power Supply Connectivity
This section provide details about power and ground isolation, as well as on-chip supply decoupling if 
indicated after running power integrity simulations.
8.5.1 Power and Ground Isolation
Power supply connectivity must be designed accounting for all noise sources, decap, parasitics, package, 
and so on. You must perform a power integrity analysis.
To prevent power supply noise from inducing jitter on the transmitted and received waveforms, ensure that 
the PHY power supplies are isolated from the power supplies used on the rest of the ASIC. 
The PHY provides power pins for purpose of additional on-die decap, if needed, as determined by your 
power integrity analysis of the PHY plus the package plus the board. Due to jitter and isolation requirement 
these pins should not be used to connect the PHY to other on-die supplies.
The PHY ground, gd, is isolated on the die from the ASIC core ground gddig. Back-to-back diodes between 
the ASIC ground (connected to gddig pin) and the PHY ground, gd, are provided within the PHY macro as 
part of the ESD protection strategy (described in “ESD Protection Strategy” on page 141“), but no direct 
metal-metal connection is permitted.
Isolation of all power and ground on the die implies the only connection between the PHY hard macro and 
the rest of the ASIC are for signal wires. The signal integrity of this interface is maintained using a fully 
clock-forwarded architecture where both clock and data cross the power domains together, so that subtle 
differences in supply voltages have no impact on timing.
8.5.2 On-Chip Supply Decoupling
If power integrity simulations indicate that additional PHY power supply decoupling is needed, one option 
is to include that capacitance on-chip. This may also be advantageous for utilizing space in between PHY 
instantiations or along the die-edge.
Contact Synopsys for on-chip capacitance addition to ensure proper decoupling capacitance placement and 
connection to listed power supplies.
Note Power integrity of the macro supplies must be considered early in the design process and can 
impact both on-die implementation (if additional on-die decap is needed) as well as decap 
design and board and package implementation.

8.6 ESD
This section presents ESD considerations.
8.6.1 ESD Requirements
ESD performance is evaluated by the amount of voltage and current that the protection circuits can absorb 
during an ESD event while keeping voltages below damaging levels. The protection circuits are designed to 
meet the protection levels provided in Table 8-4.
Table 8-4 Minimum ESD Protection Level
8.6.2 ESD Protection Strategy
The ESD protection strategy of the PHY provides the following features:
■ Large primary diodes between signal pad and PHY power and ground supplies
■ A series resistor and secondary diodes between signal pad and PHY power and ground supplies
■ An active clamp between the PHY power and ground supplies
The clamp and primary diodes are placed close to each other within the PHY, minimizing the resis-
tance between them and maximizing the effectiveness of the ESD circuits.
■ Back-to-back diodes between PHY ground, gd, and interfacing (ASIC) ground, gddig
■ Sufficient metal width in all ESD paths
Figure 8-5 shows the recommended ESD strategy to implement in
Figure 8-5 ESD Protection Strategy
During ESD events, the primary diodes direct most of the current to the PHY power/ground buses and 
through the clamp. This path has minimal resistance, keeping the signal pad voltage and voltage across the 
PHY power buses low. Some current flows through the secondary diodes and develops a further voltage

The back-to-back diodes prevent damage between interfacing circuits due to power bus differences.
8.6.3 ESD Protection Stra tegy for Die-to-Die Pins
The ESD protection strategy of the PHY provides the following features:
■ Current path between signal pad and PHY power and ground supplies
■ Back-to-back diodes between PHY ground, gd, and interfacing (ASIC) ground, gddig
■ Sufficient metal width in all ESD paths
8.6.4 ESD Back-to-Back Diode  Connection to Core VSS
Although gddig and gd are to be connected together within the package, for ESD protection of the 
PHY/ASIC interface a back to back diode connection between gddig and gd is provided within the PHY.
The gddig pin on the PHY must have a strong connection to the ASIC gddig supply grid. The gddig pin 
provided in the IP should be connected with a solid metal. There should be less than 0.5 ohm of resistance 
between the gddig pin on the PHY and the closest clamp within the ASIC (see Figure 8-6).
Figure 8-6 Back-to-Back Diodes in PHY
If non-ground supplies are connected together for ESD protection through back-to-back diodes, special care 
must be taken to check for, and guard against latch-up, power-up sequencing and leakage issues.
Note Add a vdd-to-vss clamp close to the back-to-back diodes with no more than 0.5 ohm routing 
resistance from the diodes to the clamp or with a value recommended by the foundry Design 
Rule Manual (DRM).

8.7 Reference Resistor
An external reference resistor is used for calibration of the RX and TX termination. The external resistor is 
automatically used for calibration when the PHY comes out of reset and when a manual tuning request is 
made. There is also the possibility to demand a manual tuning request. When a PHY is using the reference 
resistor, it forces current across the resistor. When a PHY is not using the reference resistor, the current is 
disabled.

8.8 Raw PCS Synthesis
The raw PCS is delivered as soft RTL, it will be hardened along with the rest of the RTL of the protocol-PCS 
controller and ASIC. Synthesizing the raw PCS requires a Verilog-2001 compatible synthesis tool.
8.8.1 Synthesis Database
The deliverables include the Design Compiler synthesis scripts in TCL format that can be used to synthesize 
the raw PCS soft macro logic using the timing model of the PMA. There are two PMAs and two timing 
models available.

8.8.2 Raw PCS Constraints
The synthesis constraints are provided in the form of TCL scripts used by Synopsys Design Compiler. These 
files (./Latest/macro/constraints/) describe the design intent and surrounding constraints for synthesis, 
clocking, timing, power, test, and environmental and operating conditions. The constraints include the 
input and output delays of interface pins, clock definitions, and any timing path exceptions in the design, 
such as false paths or multi-cycle paths.
In most cases, the raw PCS is synthesized in conjunction with other logic that resides on the opposite side of 
the ASIC interface boundary. In this situation, timing constraints can be automatically inferred from the 
adjoining logic and from the PMA timing model to which the raw PCS is connected. When synthesizing the 
raw PCS as a standalone block, signals on the ASIC interface must be constrained, so that sufficient time is 
budgeted for the adjoining logic to be connected later.
8.8.2.1 Hand-Placed Logic Constraints
There are primitive modules within the raw PCS that are used to instantiate a specific digital cell that must 
not be logically optimized during synthesis. These modules are typically used around asynchronous Clock 
Domain Crossing (CDC) points within the design. It is essential that these modules are processed correctly, 
not just synthesized as usual with the raw PCS logic. Failure to do so will likely create glitch-generating and 
re-convergent logic that can cause problems on the ASIC interface.
Table 8-8 lists the raw PCS primitive modules.
Table 8-8 Raw PCS Primitive Modules

*_test.serial.tdf.v)
■ Top-level Verilog ATPG parallel testbench (*_test.para.tdf.v and

raw PCS synthesis.

The clock definitions and associated false paths for the PHY are provided in the constraints folder. 
The PHY supports two scan clock modes for stuck-at and at-speed (ASST) scan.
■ scan_clk_sel=1
The scan clock MUX selects the scan_clk input clock. This clock is used by entire PHY. Only stuck-at 
(shift and capture) is supported.
■ scan_clk_sel=0 
The scan clock MUX selects the at-speed scan input clocks. Both stuck-at and at speed (shift and 
capture) are supported. The scan clock inputs are required to be one-hot.
The scan clock MUX is not glitchless. So scan_clk_sel should not change dynamically when the clocks are 
active.

8.8.2.3 Syncronizers
Raw PCS instantiates the synchronizer modules used for clock domain crossing protection. The design 
includes two types of synchronizers, data and reset synchronizers.
Data synchronizers are used for single-bit signals that must be passed from one clock domain to another, an 
instance of one of the modules *_xsr_sync is used to synchronize the source signal into the receiving clock 
domain.
Figure 8-15 shows the structure of a single-bit 3-stage data synchronizer.
Figure 8-15 dwc_*_*_xsr_sync Module
The input to the synchronizer first stage can be considered completely asynchronous. All paths to the flop1 
register can be considered false_paths. The second and third registers in the dwc_*xsr_sync module are 
flop2 and flop3. To ensure stable synchronization, constrain the maximum delay to these cells, from 
flop1/Q to flop2/D and from flop2/Q to flop3/D to 20% of the sampling clock frequency. For ease of 
timing constraint definition, this delay can be implemented as 200 ps (20% of 1000 MHz) plus the intended 
clock tree depth of the respective sampling clock for the associated module instances. You can ignore the 
clock-tree delay if the paths are constrained explicitly from flop1/Q to flop2/D and from flop2/Q to 
flop3/D. Metastability behavior is not modeled by default in the synchronizers (for RTL simulations only). 
This behavior can be enabled by defining the macro VERIFY_EN.

cpu_jtag_tck_i 100

154
Reset Synchronizers are used for synchronizing the asynchronous reset signal, an instance of the modules, 
dwc_*_param_rstgen is used. As shown in Figure 8-16, any time an external or internal reset condition is 
passed from one clock domain to another domain, the reset condition must pass through 
dwc_*xsr_*_param_rstgen module to do the following:
■ Asynchronously capture the reset signal
■ Stretch the reset pulse to be active for at least one full clock cycle in the receiving domain 
■ Synchronously de-assert the reset signal with respect to the receiving domain
Because assertion of a reset will cause all flip-flops in the synchronizer module to capture the pulse, the 
de-assertion edge of the asynchronous reset input has no effect on the final flip-flop that drives the reset into 
the receiving domain. All asynchronous paths through the d_s3 register of this module can be considered 
false. 
Figure 8-16 dwc_ *_*_param_rstgen
8.8.2.4 txX_clk Constraints 
However, it is possible for the incoming ASIC txX_clk to drift relative to the internal txX_ana_dword_clk 
domain because of voltage and temperature variation. Because TX clock alignment is performed only on 
initial phase for FW. The variation on the txX_clk must be bound to ensure that this drift does not cause 
txX_clk to misalign relative to the txX_ana_dword_clk domain, resulting in the PHY’s inability to capture 
incoming data. Because the ASIC txX_clk is derived from the output PMA MPLL clocks, the entire path 
from the PMA lib outputs, through the external logic and back to the PMA, the txX_clk input must be 
analyzed to determine the worst-case clock tree latency variation across all voltage/temperature (VT) 
corners.
This external variation must be less than 100ps when PMA internal TX FIFO is disabled, and less than (0.5 
WORD clock + 100ps) ~400ps when FIFO is enabled.
By default the FIFO datapath is disabled because it causes latency increasing.
8.8.2.5 PMA .lib ETM Timing Modes
The deliverables include multi-mode .lib files for the PMA modules (TX and RX) that provide timing 
information for various modes of operation.

end aggressors).

The following are DC limit recommendations:
■ The overshoot or undershoot of the low-frequency supply caused due to the board filter network 
should be lower than 100 kHz.
■ The overall DC budget should account for the low frequency overshoot/undershoot in addition to the 
board plus package IR drop.
■ There is a transition zone between 10 kHz and 100 kHz frequencies in which the supply noise can 
increase as the frequency decreases at a rate of 20 dB/Dec up to 5% of VPH.
The following are AC limit recommendations:
■ Use an LDO as a switching supply.
■ When using the switching supply for the VPH, its tone and harmonics are within the more sensitive 
region. Make sure that you meet the tighter noise limits.
■ Do not share directly with other noisy circuitry.

9.3.2 Package Guidelines – Avoidance of  Substrate Routing Above Inductors
There are on-die inductors implemented in the PHY: Tcoil inductors in transmit and receive paths, and in 
MPLL. In the silicon interposer or package layout, Synopsys requires that you avoid any routing of signals, 
for example, TX or Rx differential lines or reference clock lines, or any other toggling signals immediately 
beneath the MPLL on-chip inductors due to noise coupling concerns. Synopsys recommends to have a 
ground metal reference copper or an IO Supply reference copper populated under MPLL inductor at 50 um 
or more vertical spacing on package substrate or interposer. This helps to shield inductor from noise 
coupling and at the same time ensure on chip inductance not affected.
Figure 9-5 shows the inductor location within the common module (CM). For the coordinates of the 
keep-out areas illustrated in Figure 8-3 on page 136 and Figure 8-4 on page 137, contact Synopsys.

10.1 Clocking Architecture
High-speed clock is generated by the common clock module in the support block (SUP). The support clock 
consists of two PLL’s MPLL and ring-oscillator phase-locked loop. 
MPLL generates clock from 4.6875–5.5296 GHz. ROPLL uses divided clock (divide by 5) and can support 
frequencies from 7-14 GHz. 
The high-frequency reference clock allows a high ROPLL bandwidth to optimize the jitter compared to 
power performance of the ROPLL. 
The MPLL generates the high-frequency clocks used by the ROPLL which are subsequently used by lanes 
(TX and RX). MPLL clock used in TX can be send to RX in clock forwarded configuration (see Figure 10-1).
Figure 10-1 RX and TX Configuration for Clock Forwarded Mode
You need to configure the MPLL, so that it runs at the correct frequency for your application and protocols.
To configure the MPLL use Appendix A, “PHY Configuration” to determine the correct setting of the 
mpll_multiplier[11:0]. The divider has a resolution of 0.25 input clock cycles and thus mpll_multiplier[0] has 
a binary weighting of 0.25, mpll_multiplier[1] has a weighting of 0.5, mpll_multiplier[2] has a weighting of 
1, and so on. 
Use the following formulas to better understand how to configure MPLL and ROPLL frequencies.
MPLL Configuration
N = 5*mplla_ana_word_clk_div5_en+4*mplla_ana_word_clk_div4_en
For RX in clock forwarding mode the same Fropll_refclk is used to define FVCO Frequency of RX Ropll.

10.3 Reference Clock Implementation
There are two types of reference clock implementations:
■ External off-chip reference clocks using ref_pad_clk_p/ref_pad_clk_m 
■ On-chip reference clocks using ref_alt_clk_p/m
Both reference clock implementations support either a differential clock or a CMOS single-ended clock. The 
reference clock input is an unterminated AC coupled input. 
If the reference clock is a single-ended CMOS clock, one of the clock inputs (such as ref_pad_clk_p) must be 
a full-swing CMOS clock, and the other input (such as ref_pad_clk_m) should be tied to either high or low. 
If the reference clock is differential, it may be CML or LVDS, or any differential specification that meets the 
requirements outlined in Table 10-1.
Any reference clock inputs that are not used should be tied to ground.
10.3.1 External Off- Chip Clock Sources
When routing reference clocks in from off-chip, a signal integrity (SI) analysis should be performed using a 
model of the clock input, package, board traces, and clock source. This analysis should confirm that the 
reference clock input specification is met across the external on-chip reference clock input 
(ref_pad_clk_p/ref_pad_clk_m), as outlined in “Reference Clock Requirements” on page 167. Figure 10-2

10.3.2 On-Chip Clock Sources
Many applications require an on-chip reference clock. The challenge is to get the reference clock to the PHY 
without adding supply-noise-induced jitter to the clock.
The following general guidelines should be followed when routing an on-chip reference clock:
■ Route manually (do not use PNR tool router)
■ Design differential routing to optimize RC
■ Consider higher-level metal to get best RC factor (lower-level metals tend to be too resistive)
■ Use shielding on both sides of diff routes
■ Avoid noisy signals directly above and below the routes
■ If you do route signals near the routes, do not have them parallel—use perpen dicular routes only, so 
that noise is common mode
■ Simulate
Figure 10-4 shows a model of the reference clock input with parameters provided in Table 10-4.
When routing reference clocks on-chip, you should consider the parasitic loading of the clock distribution 
lines and the PHY clock input. This analysis should confirm that the reference clock input specification is 
met across the on-chip reference clock input (ref_alt_clk_p0/m and ref_alt_clk_p1/m) as outlined in 
“Reference Clock Requirements” on page 167.

10.3.3 Reference Clock Repeater
There is a reference clock repeater output (ref_repeat_clk_m/ref_repeat_clk_p) that allows the PHY to 
buffer the reference clock and send it on to another PHY. The driver is a VP supply referenced CML output 
clock driver, as shown in Figure 10-5. The repeater is designed to meet the specifications given in “Repeat 
Clock Specification” on page 168.
The following general guidelines should be followed when routing the repeater output 
(ref_repeat_clk_p/ref_repeat_clk_m) from one PHY to another PHY.
■ Route manually (do not use PNR tool router) with following recommended configuration:
❑ Signal routing width = 1 µm, highest metal
❑ Spacing (ref_repeat_clk_p - ref_repeat_clk_m) = 2 µm
❑ Spacing (signal-to-shield) = 2.5 µm
❑ Ground Shield Width = minimum per DRC
❑ GDshield - (2.5 µm space) - ref_repeat_clk_p - (2 µm space) - ref_repeat_clk_m - (2.5 µm space) - 
Gdshield
❑ Density layer fill for above and below routes (that is, +1/-1 of routing layer)
■ Design differential routing to optimize RC.
■ Consider the highest-level metal to get the best RC factor (lower-level metals tend to be too resistive).
■ Use shielding on both sides of differential routes. 
■ Avoid noisy signals directly above and below the routes.
■ If you do route signals near the routes, do not have them parallel. Use perpendicular routes only, so 
that noise is common mode and has a minimal impact on the differential signal.
■ Simulate.
Ref_clk repeat buffer and single-ended buffers are not in an always-on power domain. If powergating is 
enabled, these outputs are powered down. Do not use these signals for logic or PHYs intended to remain 
operational during power-gating.
Within prescaler: 3-1 multiplexer turn-on resistor R3 150 ohm
Within prescaler: AC cap C3 880 fF
Within prescaler: DC bias resistor R4 460 kohm
Within prescaler: input cap of prescaler input RX C4 <= 120 fF
Note To improve common-mode rejection ratio (CMRR), differential traces should be as well 
matched as possible.

11
Register Descriptions
This chapter details all possible registers in the IP. They are arranged hierarchically into maps and blocks 
(banks).
Exists Expression
The Exist expressions (if present) indicate the combination of configuration parameters required for a 
register, field, or block to exist in the memory map. The expression is only valid in the local context and does 
not indicate the conditions for existence of the parent. For example, the Exists expression for a bit field in a 
register assumes that the register exists and does not include the conditions for existence of the register.
Offset
The term Offset is synonymous with Address.
Memory Access Attributes
The Memory Access attribute is defined as <ReadBehavior>/<WriteBehavior> which are defined in the 
following table.
Table 11-1 Possible Read and Write Behaviors
Read (or Write) Behavior Description
RC A read clears this register field.
RS A read sets this register field.
RM A read modifies the contents of this register field.
Wo You can only write once to this register field.
W1C A write of 1 clears this register field.
W1S A write of 1 sets this register field.
W1T A write of 1 toggles this register field.
W0C A write of 0 clears this register field.
W0S A write of 0 sets this register field.

W0T A write of 0 toggles this register field.

R Read-only register field.
